7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Analytical Problem ID: LAC-Q24

Change the order of integration and evaluate: int_0^a int_y^a x / (x^2 + y^2) dx dy. Change the order of integration and evaluate: int_0^a int_y^a x / (x^2 + y^2) dx dy. Change the order of integration and evaluate: int_0^a int_y^a x / (x^2 + y^2) dx dy.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\int_0^a \int_y^a \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dx \, dy = \int_0^a \left[\int_0^x \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dy \right] dx = \int_0^a \frac{\pi}{4} \, dx = \frac{\pi a}{4}$$

Change of Order for Triangular Domain: Switching order from $dx\,dy$ to $dy\,dx$ converts the complicated logarithmic $x$-integral into a standard inverse tangent $\tan^{-1}(y/x)$, collapsing the inner integral to constant $\pi/4$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\int_0^a \int_y^a \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dx \, dy = \int_0^a \left[\int_0^x \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dy \right] dx = \int_0^a \frac{\pi}{4} \, dx = \frac{\pi a}{4}$$

Change of Order for Triangular Domain: Switching order from $dx\,dy$ to $dy\,dx$ converts the complicated logarithmic $x$-integral into a standard inverse tangent $\tan^{-1}(y/x)$, collapsing the inner integral to constant $\pi/4$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\int_0^a \int_y^a \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dx \, dy = \int_0^a \left[\int_0^x \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dy \right] dx = \int_0^a \frac{\pi}{4} \, dx = \frac{\pi a}{4}$$

Change of Order for Triangular Domain: Switching order from $dx\,dy$ to $dy\,dx$ converts the complicated logarithmic $x$-integral into a standard inverse tangent $\tan^{-1}(y/x)$, collapsing the inner integral to constant $\pi/4$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to change the order of integration and evaluate the double integral: $$I = \int_{y=0}^a \int_{x=y}^a \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dx \, dy$$

**Step 1: Analyze the Original Limits and Geometry of Region $R$** In the given order $dx \, dy$: - Horizontal strips are utilized. - Inner limits: $x$ varies from the lower straight line $x = y$ to the vertical line $x = a$. - Outer limits: $y$ varies from the horizontal axis $y = 0$ to $y = a$. Geometrically, this represents a right-angled triangular domain bounded by: 1. The line $y = 0$ ($x$-axis) 2. The line $x = a$ (vertical line) 3. The line $y = x$ (diagonal line of slope 1) The vertices of this triangle are located at $(0, 0), (a, 0),$ and $(a, a)$.

**Step 2: Reverse the Order of Integration ($dy \, dx$)** To reverse the order of integration, we slice the triangular region using vertical strips parallel to the $y$-axis: - Each vertical strip enters the region from the bottom along the line $y = 0$. - The vertical strip exits the region at the top along the line $y = x$. Hence, for a fixed value of $x$, $y$ varies from $y = 0$ to $y = x$. - The horizontal span of the triangle runs from $x = 0$ to $x = a$. Hence, $x$ varies from $x = 0$ to $x = a$.

The integral with changed order becomes: $$I = \int_{x=0}^a \left[ \int_{y=0}^x \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dy \right] dx$$

**Step 3: Evaluate the Inner Integral with respect to $y$** In the inner integral, $x$ is held constant. Recall the standard standard inverse-tangent integral formula: $$\int \frac{1}{y^2 + x^2} \, dy = \frac{1}{x} \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{y}{x} \right)$$ Multiplying by the constant numerator $x$: $$\int_{y=0}^x \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dy = x \left[ \frac{1}{x} \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{y}{x} \right) \right]_{y=0}^{y=x} = \left[ \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{y}{x} \right) \right]_{y=0}^{y=x}$$ Evaluate at the limits: $$= \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{x}{x} \right) - \tan^{-1}(0) = \tan^{-1}(1) - 0 = \frac{\pi}{4}$$ The inner integral evaluates to the constant $\frac{\pi}{4}$, completely independent of $x$.

**Step 4: Evaluate the Outer Integral with respect to $x$** Substitute the result of the inner integral: $$I = \int_{x=0}^a \frac{\pi}{4} \, dx = \frac{\pi}{4} [x]_0^a = \frac{\pi}{4} (a - 0) = \frac{\pi a}{4}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

We are required to change the order of integration and evaluate the double integral: $$I = \int_{y=0}^a \int_{x=y}^a \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dx \, dy$$

**Step 1: Analyze the Original Limits and Geometry of Region $R$** In the given order $dx \, dy$: - Horizontal strips are utilized. - Inner limits: $x$ varies from the lower straight line $x = y$ to the vertical line $x = a$. - Outer limits: $y$ varies from the horizontal axis $y = 0$ to $y = a$. Geometrically, this represents a right-angled triangular domain bounded by: 1. The line $y = 0$ ($x$-axis) 2. The line $x = a$ (vertical line) 3. The line $y = x$ (diagonal line of slope 1) The vertices of this triangle are located at $(0, 0), (a, 0),$ and $(a, a)$.

**Step 2: Reverse the Order of Integration ($dy \, dx$)** To reverse the order of integration, we slice the triangular region using vertical strips parallel to the $y$-axis: - Each vertical strip enters the region from the bottom along the line $y = 0$. - The vertical strip exits the region at the top along the line $y = x$. Hence, for a fixed value of $x$, $y$ varies from $y = 0$ to $y = x$. - The horizontal span of the triangle runs from $x = 0$ to $x = a$. Hence, $x$ varies from $x = 0$ to $x = a$.

The integral with changed order becomes: $$I = \int_{x=0}^a \left[ \int_{y=0}^x \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dy \right] dx$$

**Step 3: Evaluate the Inner Integral with respect to $y$** In the inner integral, $x$ is held constant. Recall the standard standard inverse-tangent integral formula: $$\int \frac{1}{y^2 + x^2} \, dy = \frac{1}{x} \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{y}{x} \right)$$ Multiplying by the constant numerator $x$: $$\int_{y=0}^x \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dy = x \left[ \frac{1}{x} \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{y}{x} \right) \right]_{y=0}^{y=x} = \left[ \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{y}{x} \right) \right]_{y=0}^{y=x}$$ Evaluate at the limits: $$= \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{x}{x} \right) - \tan^{-1}(0) = \tan^{-1}(1) - 0 = \frac{\pi}{4}$$ The inner integral evaluates to the constant $\frac{\pi}{4}$, completely independent of $x$.

**Step 4: Evaluate the Outer Integral with respect to $x$** Substitute the result of the inner integral: $$I = \int_{x=0}^a \frac{\pi}{4} \, dx = \frac{\pi}{4} [x]_0^a = \frac{\pi}{4} (a - 0) = \frac{\pi a}{4}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

We are required to change the order of integration and evaluate the double integral: $$I = \int_{y=0}^a \int_{x=y}^a \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dx \, dy$$

**Step 1: Analyze the Original Limits and Geometry of Region $R$** In the given order $dx \, dy$: - Horizontal strips are utilized. - Inner limits: $x$ varies from the lower straight line $x = y$ to the vertical line $x = a$. - Outer limits: $y$ varies from the horizontal axis $y = 0$ to $y = a$. Geometrically, this represents a right-angled triangular domain bounded by: 1. The line $y = 0$ ($x$-axis) 2. The line $x = a$ (vertical line) 3. The line $y = x$ (diagonal line of slope 1) The vertices of this triangle are located at $(0, 0), (a, 0),$ and $(a, a)$.

**Step 2: Reverse the Order of Integration ($dy \, dx$)** To reverse the order of integration, we slice the triangular region using vertical strips parallel to the $y$-axis: - Each vertical strip enters the region from the bottom along the line $y = 0$. - The vertical strip exits the region at the top along the line $y = x$. Hence, for a fixed value of $x$, $y$ varies from $y = 0$ to $y = x$. - The horizontal span of the triangle runs from $x = 0$ to $x = a$. Hence, $x$ varies from $x = 0$ to $x = a$.

The integral with changed order becomes: $$I = \int_{x=0}^a \left[ \int_{y=0}^x \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dy \right] dx$$

**Step 3: Evaluate the Inner Integral with respect to $y$** In the inner integral, $x$ is held constant. Recall the standard standard inverse-tangent integral formula: $$\int \frac{1}{y^2 + x^2} \, dy = \frac{1}{x} \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{y}{x} \right)$$ Multiplying by the constant numerator $x$: $$\int_{y=0}^x \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} \, dy = x \left[ \frac{1}{x} \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{y}{x} \right) \right]_{y=0}^{y=x} = \left[ \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{y}{x} \right) \right]_{y=0}^{y=x}$$ Evaluate at the limits: $$= \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{x}{x} \right) - \tan^{-1}(0) = \tan^{-1}(1) - 0 = \frac{\pi}{4}$$ The inner integral evaluates to the constant $\frac{\pi}{4}$, completely independent of $x$.

**Step 4: Evaluate the Outer Integral with respect to $x$** Substitute the result of the inner integral: $$I = \int_{x=0}^a \frac{\pi}{4} \, dx = \frac{\pi}{4} [x]_0^a = \frac{\pi}{4} (a - 0) = \frac{\pi a}{4}$$

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Analytical Problem ID: LAC-Q25

Find the area bounded by the parabolas y^2 = 4ax and x^2 = 4ay using double integration. Find the area bounded by the parabolas y^2 = 4ax and x^2 = 4ay using double integration. Find the area bounded by the parabolas y^2 = 4ax and x^2 = 4ay using double integration.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\text{Area} = \int_0^{4a} \left(2\sqrt{ax} - \frac{x^2}{4a}\right) dx = \left[\frac{4\sqrt{a}}{3}x^{3/2} - \frac{x^3}{12a}\right]_0^{4a} = \frac{16a^2}{3}$$

Area Enclosed by Conjugate Parabolas: The intersection between $y^2 = 4ax$ and $x^2 = 4ay$ spans $[0, 4a]$. Double integration yields the celebrated classical area result $\frac{16a^2}{3}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\text{Area} = \int_0^{4a} \left(2\sqrt{ax} - \frac{x^2}{4a}\right) dx = \left[\frac{4\sqrt{a}}{3}x^{3/2} - \frac{x^3}{12a}\right]_0^{4a} = \frac{16a^2}{3}$$

Area Enclosed by Conjugate Parabolas: The intersection between $y^2 = 4ax$ and $x^2 = 4ay$ spans $[0, 4a]$. Double integration yields the celebrated classical area result $\frac{16a^2}{3}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\text{Area} = \int_0^{4a} \left(2\sqrt{ax} - \frac{x^2}{4a}\right) dx = \left[\frac{4\sqrt{a}}{3}x^{3/2} - \frac{x^3}{12a}\right]_0^{4a} = \frac{16a^2}{3}$$

Area Enclosed by Conjugate Parabolas: The intersection between $y^2 = 4ax$ and $x^2 = 4ay$ spans $[0, 4a]$. Double integration yields the celebrated classical area result $\frac{16a^2}{3}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to compute the area bounded between the two intersecting parabolas: $$y^2 = 4ax \quad \text{and} \quad x^2 = 4ay \quad (a > 0)$$ using double integration.

**Step 1: Find Points of Intersection** Express $y$ from the second parabola: $$y = \frac{x^2}{4a}$$ Substitute this into the equation of the first parabola: $$\left( \frac{x^2}{4a} \right)^2 = 4ax \implies \frac{x^4}{16a^2} = 4ax$$ $$x^4 = 64a^3 x \implies x^4 - 64a^3 x = 0 \implies x(x^3 - 64a^3) = 0$$ The real roots are: $$x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad x = (64a^3)^{1/3} = 4a$$

Find corresponding $y$-values: - At $x = 0$: $y = \frac{0^2}{4a} = 0 \implies (0, 0)$ (the origin). - At $x = 4a$: $y = \frac{(4a)^2}{4a} = \frac{16a^2}{4a} = 4a \implies (4a, 4a)$. The two parabolas enclose a single bounded region spanning from $x = 0$ to $x = 4a$.

**Step 2: Determine Upper and Lower Boundary Functions** For any $x \in (0, 4a)$: - Lower boundary curve: $y_1(x) = \frac{x^2}{4a}$ (from $x^2 = 4ay$) - Upper boundary curve: $y_2(x) = \sqrt{4ax} = 2\sqrt{a} \sqrt{x}$ (from $y^2 = 4ax$)

**Step 3: Set up the Double Integral for Area** $$\text{Area } A = \iint_R dy \, dx = \int_{x=0}^{4a} \left[ \int_{y=x^2/(4a)}^{2\sqrt{a}\sqrt{x}} dy \right] dx$$

**Step 4: Evaluate the Inner and Outer Integrals** Evaluate the inner integral: $$\int_{x^2/(4a)}^{2\sqrt{a}\sqrt{x}} dy = [y]_{x^2/(4a)}^{2\sqrt{a}\sqrt{x}} = 2\sqrt{a} x^{1/2} - \frac{x^2}{4a}$$

Now evaluate the outer integral with respect to $x$: $$A = \int_0^{4a} \left( 2\sqrt{a} x^{1/2} - \frac{1}{4a} x^2 \right) dx$$ $$= 2\sqrt{a} \left[ \frac{x^{3/2}}{3/2} \right]_0^{4a} - \frac{1}{4a} \left[ \frac{x^3}{3} \right]_0^{4a}$$ $$= \frac{4\sqrt{a}}{3} (4a)^{3/2} - \frac{1}{12a} (4a)^3$$

Compute each term: $$(4a)^{3/2} = (\sqrt{4a})^3 = (2\sqrt{a})^3 = 8 a\sqrt{a} = 8a^{3/2}$$ $$(4a)^3 = 64a^3$$ Substitute these values: $$A = \frac{4\sqrt{a}}{3} (8 a\sqrt{a}) - \frac{1}{12a} (64a^3)$$ $$A = \frac{32a^2}{3} - \frac{64a^2}{12} = \frac{32a^2}{3} - \frac{16a^2}{3} = \frac{16a^2}{3}$$ Thus, the enclosed area is $\frac{16a^2}{3}$ square units.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Analytical Problem ID: LAC-Q26

Find the volume of the solid bounded by the cylinder x^2 + y^2 = 4 and the planes y + z = 4 and z = 0 using triple integration. Find the volume of the solid bounded by the cylinder x^2 + y^2 = 4 and the planes y + z = 4 and z = 0 using triple integration. Find the volume of the solid bounded by the cylinder x^2 + y^2 = 4 and the planes y + z = 4 and z = 0 using triple integration.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V = \int_0^{2\pi} \int_0^2 (4 - r\sin\theta) r \, dr \, d\theta = \int_0^{2\pi} \left(8 - \frac{8}{3}\sin\theta\right) d\theta = 16\pi$$

Volume of Truncated Cylinder via Cylindrical Coordinates: Cylindrical coordinates simplify circular boundaries ($r \le 2$). The odd periodic term $\sin\theta$ integrates to zero over $[0, 2\pi]$, leaving mean height $4$ times base area $\pi(2^2) = 16\pi$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V = \int_0^{2\pi} \int_0^2 (4 - r\sin\theta) r \, dr \, d\theta = \int_0^{2\pi} \left(8 - \frac{8}{3}\sin\theta\right) d\theta = 16\pi$$

Volume of Truncated Cylinder via Cylindrical Coordinates: Cylindrical coordinates simplify circular boundaries ($r \le 2$). The odd periodic term $\sin\theta$ integrates to zero over $[0, 2\pi]$, leaving mean height $4$ times base area $\pi(2^2) = 16\pi$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V = \int_0^{2\pi} \int_0^2 (4 - r\sin\theta) r \, dr \, d\theta = \int_0^{2\pi} \left(8 - \frac{8}{3}\sin\theta\right) d\theta = 16\pi$$

Volume of Truncated Cylinder via Cylindrical Coordinates: Cylindrical coordinates simplify circular boundaries ($r \le 2$). The odd periodic term $\sin\theta$ integrates to zero over $[0, 2\pi]$, leaving mean height $4$ times base area $\pi(2^2) = 16\pi$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to determine the volume of the solid region bounded by: - The right circular cylinder: $x^2 + y^2 = 4$ - The upper inclined plane: $y + z = 4 \implies z = 4 - y$ - The lower horizontal plane: $z = 0$ using triple integration.

**Step 1: Set up the Volume Integral in Cylindrical Coordinates** Because the lateral boundary is a circular cylinder centered on the $z$-axis, cylindrical coordinates $(r, \theta, z)$ are the most natural and efficient coordinate system: $$x = r \cos \theta, \quad y = r \sin \theta, \quad z = z$$ The differential volume element in cylindrical coordinates is: $$dV = dz \, r \, dr \, d\theta$$

**Step 2: Determine Limits of Integration** - **$z$-limits:** The solid is bounded below by the plane $z = 0$ and above by the inclined plane $z = 4 - y = 4 - r \sin \theta$. Since $r \le 2$, $4 - r \sin \theta \ge 4 - 2 = 2 > 0$, guaranteeing that the upper plane always lies strictly above $z = 0$. Hence $z$ ranges from $0$ to $4 - r \sin \theta$. - **$r$-limits:** The projection of the cylinder onto the $xy$-plane is the circular disk $x^2 + y^2 \le 4$, so $r$ ranges from $0$ to $2$. - **$\theta$-limits:** The full cross-section requires tracing the complete circle, so $\theta$ ranges from $0$ to $2\pi$.

**Step 3: Evaluate the Triple Integral** $$V = \iiint_D dV = \int_{\theta=0}^{2\pi} \int_{r=0}^2 \int_{z=0}^{4 - r \sin \theta} dz \, r \, dr \, d\theta$$

Evaluate the innermost integral with respect to $z$: $$\int_{z=0}^{4 - r \sin \theta} dz = [z]_0^{4 - r \sin \theta} = 4 - r \sin \theta$$ Substitute into the double integral: $$V = \int_{\theta=0}^{2\pi} \int_{r=0}^2 (4 - r \sin \theta) r \, dr \, d\theta = \int_{\theta=0}^{2\pi} \int_{r=0}^2 (4r - r^2 \sin \theta) \, dr \, d\theta$$

Evaluate the inner integral with respect to $r$: $$\int_{r=0}^2 (4r - r^2 \sin \theta) \, dr = \left[ 2r^2 - \frac{r^3}{3} \sin \theta \right]_{r=0}^2 = 2(2^2) - \frac{2^3}{3} \sin \theta = 8 - \frac{8}{3} \sin \theta$$

Evaluate the outer integral with respect to $\theta$: $$V = \int_0^{2\pi} \left( 8 - \frac{8}{3} \sin \theta \right) d\theta = \int_0^{2\pi} 8 \, d\theta - \frac{8}{3} \int_0^{2\pi} \sin \theta \, d\theta$$ $$\int_0^{2\pi} 8 \, d\theta = [8\theta]_0^{2\pi} = 8(2\pi) = 16\pi$$ $$\int_0^{2\pi} \sin \theta \, d\theta = [-\cos \theta]_0^{2\pi} = -\cos(2\pi) - (-\cos 0) = -1 - (-1) = 0$$ Therefore: $$V = 16\pi - \frac{8}{3}(0) = 16\pi$$ The volume of the truncated cylinder is $16\pi$ cubic units.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

We are required to determine the volume of the solid region bounded by: - The right circular cylinder: $x^2 + y^2 = 4$ - The upper inclined plane: $y + z = 4 \implies z = 4 - y$ - The lower horizontal plane: $z = 0$ using triple integration.
